# External Description of the Volume

**Condition Note:**
The text on the spine of the book is heavily faded and largely illegible due to significant wear and loss of gilding <note>The process of applying gold leaf for decoration</note>.

**Binding and Materials:**
This manuscript is housed in an antique **half-leather binding** <term>half-leather binding<gloss>A binding technique where the spine and corners are covered in leather for durability, while the sides are covered in a different material like paper or cloth.</gloss></term>. The spine and corners are made of dark brown leather, contrasted by tan-colored paper or **vellum** <term>vellum<gloss>A high-quality parchment made from calfskin, often used for covers or writing surfaces.</gloss></term> covering the **boards** <term>boards<gloss>The stiff front and back panels that form the core of the book cover.</gloss></term>. 

The leather shows significant scuffing, cracking, and a loss of its original finish along the edges and the spine. The spine is decorated with horizontal **gold-tooled** <term>gold-tooling<gloss>A decorative technique where heated brass tools are used to press gold leaf into the leather.</gloss></term> bands. 

A small, rectangular green label is affixed to the spine. It bears faded gold lettering that is difficult to decipher, though it appears to indicate a volume number, possibly **"Vol. II"**. The tan surfaces of the boards are stained and mottled with age, including several dark spots and areas where the surface layer has peeled away.

# Preface.

Man is born with an irresistible inclination toward the **knowledge of nature**; a constant striving for it operates within his spirit. If he wishes to reach fulfillment, he must seek to investigate **causes** through their **effects**.

Can he possibly perceive too early the course that nature takes and the **laws** she follows, in order to fathom the means of tracing her **fundamental laws**? — Yet man does exactly the opposite; he allows himself to be [led] by the <note>The text cuts off here with the prefix "ge-". Based on the context of Enlightenment-era philosophy, this likely intended to say "common prejudices" (gemeinen Vorurtheilen) or "accustomed paths" (gewohnten Gängen).</note>

<vocab>Knowledge, Nature, Causes, Effects, Laws, Fundamental Laws, Striving</vocab>

<summary>This preface introduces the philosophical necessity of studying natural science, arguing that humans have an innate drive to understand the laws and causes behind natural phenomena.</summary>

<keywords>natural philosophy, epistemology, causation, laws of nature, human nature, Enlightenment</keywords>

most common prejudices; he falls for the deceptive **marvelous**, and falls into all manner of **illusions** through which he allows himself to be deceived. <note>The author is likely referring to the contemporary fascination with "wonders" or superstitions that distract from the rational study of physical laws.</note>

He occupies himself solely with what most immediately surrounds him—a miserable source from which to draw the **knowledge of the nature of his being**! <note>The author suggests that looking only at our immediate, everyday experiences is insufficient for understanding our true nature.</note> Admittedly, this knowledge is not easily attained, for what I intend to demonstrate here is of immense scope. This **science** <note>The term 'Wissenschaft' (science) here refers to the systematic study of natural philosophy or the laws of existence.</note> has many classifications and levels; the most sharp-witted mind should not flatter itself that it has reached the final one, yet **reason** may well set foot upon the first.

I am not so conceited as to imagine that I have climbed the final step, but I flatter myself that I shall put my reader in the position—

# Preface

The workings of nature are simple, yet the systems of <term>naturalists</term> <note>original: 'Naturkündiger'. A term for those who study the physical world and its laws; in this era, it combined elements of what we now call biology, physics, and natural philosophy.</note> are complex: how can the two ever align? And yet, suffering humanity must expect its greatest aid from the experiences of scholars and from the truth of their observations. Man requires nourishment; the earth produces it—more or less—in proportion to the care and cultivation that he

<note>The text ends mid-sentence here; the catchword for the next page is "thereupon" (darauf).</note>

**Vocabulary:**
Preface, Naturalists, Humanity, Aid, Experiences, Scholars, Nourishment, Proportion

<meta>signature: A 5</meta>
<meta>catchword: thereupon</meta>

<summary>This page begins the preface by contrasting the simplicity of nature with the complexity of scientific systems, arguing that human well-being depends on scholarly observation and the diligent cultivation of the earth.</summary>
<keywords>nature, naturalists, philosophy of science, scholarship, agriculture, human needs, observation</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page, completing the thought that human existence and the production of nourishment depend on the care bestowed upon the earth. It continues the Preface (Vorbericht).</meta>

bestows upon it; can he ever delve too deeply into the **root causes** of such a necessary **production**? What science deserves more of his full attention than the one upon which his **existence** depends? To these reflections—so beneficial to the common good—we undoubtedly owe the many writings that have appeared on <term>medical science</term> <note>original: 'Arzneywissenschaft'. This term refers to the systematic study of medicine and remedies.</note> since the time of **Hippocrates** <note>An ancient Greek physician (c. 460 – c. 370 BC) often referred to as the "Father of Medicine."</note>; yet what benefit has truly been gained from them? Some have devised a thousand **artificial theories** that have served no purpose other than to further confuse the whole; others invented countless means to uncover their **mechanism**, without concerning themselves with whether the **laws of nature** would even conform to their **hypotheses**.

<meta>catchword: Was (What)</meta>

<keywords>Hippocrates, medical science, laws of nature, root causes, philosophy of medicine, scientific hypotheses</keywords>

Only at the true source, if one returns to it, can one be certain of finding **Nature**, following her course, and fulfilling her intentions and her interpretations; but that is exactly what our modern physicians, since the time of the great **Hippocrates** <note>The author views Hippocrates not just as an ancient authority, but as the last physician to truly observe nature without the bias of complex, modern theories.</note>, have completely neglected. ### Fundamental Causes of Nature.

By the word **Nature** one understands the entire world-building, which consists of heaven and earth, along with everything that depends upon them; this is the object of true world-wisdom <note>original: 'Welt-Weisheit'. In the 18th century, this term was synonymous with philosophy or the comprehensive study of the universe.</note>, and that which a naturalist <term>naturalist</term> <note>original: 'Naturkündiger'. A person who studies or is an expert in the natural sciences and the laws of the physical world.</note> should know: knowing means nothing else than to recognize things that surround us from their cause and from their fundamental essence <term>fundamental essence</term> <note>original: 'Grundwesenheit'. The intrinsic, core nature of a substance or being.</note>; consequently, a nature researcher must apply all his effort and labor to the investigation of the causes and the primary beings <term>primary beings</term> <note>original: 'Urwesen'. In this context, these are the primordial, essential forces or substances that constitute the foundation of the material world.</note> of nature, and what takes place within her.

<vocab>Nature, World-building, Wisdom, Naturalist, Fundamental Essence, Nature Researcher, Primary Beings</vocab>

<summary>This page functions as the title page and introduction to the treatise, defining "Nature" as the totality of the universe and establishing that true scientific knowledge requires understanding the primordial causes and essential natures of all things.</summary>
<keywords>Nature, world-wisdom, natural philosophy, primary beings, causality, naturalist, fundamental essence</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the systematic exploration of natural philosophy introduced on the previous page. The author moves from defining "Nature" to defining the relationship between "Cause" and "Primary Being," setting the metaphysical foundation for the work. The catchword at the bottom of the previous page was "Ich" (I), which begins the first sentence here. The catchword at the bottom of this page is "ganzen" (whole/entire).</meta>

Ich will not engage in any extensive investigation here as to whether a distinction exists between the cause and the **primary being** <term>primary being</term> <note>original: 'Urwesen'. In this context, it refers to a primordial or foundational entity that exists by its own nature.</note>, for I, for my part, believe that everything that is a primary being is at the same time a cause in its own way, of that which it is the **primary matter** <term>primary matter</term> <note>original: 'Urstoff'. The fundamental, raw substance from which all things are composed.</note>, or the primary being itself. No one doubts that everything which is a cause is also at the same time a primary being.

The relationship and the precise connection of the cause with its **effect** <term>effect</term> <note>original: 'Wirkung'. The result or product brought about by a cause.</note> does not permit one to form a true concept of the former without taking the latter into account. The most eminent of all causes is called the *self-acting* one in the **technical language of the philosophers** <term>technical language of the philosophers</term> <note>original: 'Kunstsprache der Weltweisen'. 'Weltweisen' or 'world-sages' was the standard 18th-century German term for philosophers or scientists.</note>, because in the truest sense it alone has an effect, although this can happen in various ways.

If this cause acts through an independent property, it is called a <term>fundamental cause</term> <note>original: 'Grundursache'.</note>; but if it acts only by means of an external cause, it is an <term>auxiliary cause</term> <note>original: 'behülfliche [Ursache]'. A secondary cause that assists a primary one.</note>.

One also distinguishes the <term>general cause</term> <note>original: 'allgemeine Ursache'.</note>, which produces several effects. Finally, a well-grounded distinction is made between the <term>first cause</term> <note>original: 'erste Ursache'. In classical philosophy and theology, this refers to the ultimate source of all existence.</note>, who is the author and creator of the

<vocab>Cause, Primary Being, Effect, Philosophers, Fundamental Cause, Auxiliary Cause, Creator, Primary Matter</vocab>

<summary>The author argues that all primary beings are inherently causes and explores the philosophical terminology used to categorize different types of causes, such as fundamental, auxiliary, and general causes.</summary>
<keywords>causality, primary being (Urwesen), effect (Wirkung), philosophy, fundamental cause, auxiliary cause, first cause, primary matter (Urstoff)</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of causes. The previous page ended mid-sentence describing how a cause acts "by means of..." (likely 'means of the whole of nature'). The catchword 'ganzen' (whole) from the previous page correctly aligns with the first word of this page. This section transitions from general definitions of causality to a specific proof for the existence of a 'First Primary Being'.</meta>

...whole of nature, which encompasses within itself the secondary causes <term>secondary causes</term> <note>original: 'zweiten Ursachen'. In traditional philosophy, these are created things that have the power to cause change, but only because they have been given that power by a primary cause.</note>, which are all created things.

One also observes in the self-acting cause, when it is precisely engaged in its activity, several things inseparable from one another that belong to the inner nature of this acting being. These are: the cause; the property that determines it to act; the action itself that occurs therein; the resulting effect that springs from it; and finally the object from which, through which, or in which this effect is produced.

## Concerning the First Primary Being

That a first cause, or a **first primary being** <term>primary being</term> <note>original: 'Urwesen'. A foundational entity or primordial being that exists of its own necessity.</note>, must exist in nature is patently true. It is, at the same time, so necessary that one can presuppose it as an undeniable truth that proves itself and is subject to no doubt at all, as I believe I shall demonstrate here.

To this end, I assume a truth that is recognized by the whole world, and which no one can deny, unless one willfully wishes to be considered a fool or a simpleton.

<vocab>Nature, Cause, Created things (Creatures), Effect/Action, Being, Primary being, Truth, Fool, Simpleton</vocab>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for the existence of a 'First Primary Being' introduced on the previous page. The text moves from the general assertion that a first cause exists to a specific logical proof derived from human consciousness and the contingency of our own existence. It concludes by beginning a critique of the Aristotelian view of an eternal universe.</meta>

This truth, which shall serve as my fundamental principle to prove the existence of a **first cause**, is inferred from our own existence. Nothing is so obviously true and certain as the proposition that we are in the world; the testimony of our senses vouches for this truth, and everything that we think, speak, and do does not permit us to believe that our existence is a delusion. It is therefore certain—indeed, more than certain—that we exist. 

However, we can exist neither from ourselves, nor through ourselves, nor by chance, nor in an accidental or necessary way; we must, therefore, exist through the mediation and by the grace of Another, who must be the author and the free **primary being** <term>primary being</term> <note>original: 'Urwesen'. A term used in 18th-century philosophy to denote the original, self-caused source of all other life and matter.</note> of the existence we possess. Thus, the first cause, which some wish to deny, is here grounded upon the truth of our very existence.

Aristotle, who teaches the eternity of the world and the immortality of the soul, allows for a truly infinite element in nature; if the world has existed from eternity, then it had no beginning, and this first cause is then unnecessary and dispensable. Consequently, this is a contradiction, because the very same reason that proves a **non-beginning** <term>non-beginning</term> <note>original: 'Unbeginn'. This refers to the concept of 'beginninglessness,' or the state of having no point of origin in time.</note> of the world, simultaneously [proves] the existence...

<vocab>Daseyn (Existence/Being), erste Ursache (First cause), Aristoteles (Aristotle), Ewigkeit der Welt (Eternity of the world), Unsterblichkeit der Seele (Immortality of the soul), Urwesen (Primary being), Unbeginn (Beginninglessness)</vocab>

<summary>The author argues that the existence of a First Cause is proven by the fact that humans exist but are not self-created. He also begins to challenge Aristotle’s theory of an eternal world, suggesting that such a view creates a logical contradiction regarding the necessity of a first cause.</summary>
<keywords>existence, First Cause, Aristotle, eternity of the world, primary being, cosmology, human consciousness, causality</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical proof for a "first cause" of the universe. The previous page ended mid-sentence, arguing that the same logic used to disprove an eternal world simultaneously proves the existence of a primary being. This page concludes that argument and begins defining the attributes of this being, such as perfection and infinity.</meta>

...the existence of a first **primary being** <term>primary being</term> <note>original: 'Urwesen'. A philosophical term for the fundamental, original entity from which all other things originate.</note> is proven, and the same cause that demonstrates this **existence** <term>existence</term> <note>original: 'Existenz'</note> allows us to recognize that the world began at some point in time, and that it has not existed from eternity.

The opposition and the relationship that necessarily exist between the primary being that acts and the object upon which it acts produce a necessary distinction.

These proofs sufficiently convince those who possess the natural light of **reason** <term>reason</term> <note>original: 'Vernunft'. In the 18th century, "the light of reason" referred to the human capacity to discover truth through logic and observation without relying solely on religious revelation.</note>; for it would be ridiculous to seriously claim that we ourselves are the cause of our own **being** <term>being</term> <note>original: 'Daseyn'</note>. It would clearly follow from such a claim that we existed before we even were, that we gave ourselves what we did not yet have, and that the effect and the cause were one and the same thing—which is a logical impossibility.

Those who are convinced of the existence of a first cause must attribute to it all possible **perfections** <term>perfections</term> <note>original: 'Vollkommenheiten'. In metaphysical terms, these are the highest possible qualities or attributes a being can possess.</note> according to the full scope of its **essence** <term>essence</term> <note>original: 'Wesenheit'</note>. It is, therefore, not only the most eminent and the most perfect cause, but it must also necessarily be infinite in its essence; consequently, it must possess within its **unity** <term>unity</term> <note>original: 'Einheit'</note> all prop- <note>The text ends here with a partial word. The catchword 'schaf-' suggests the next page begins with 'Eigenschaften,' meaning 'properties' or 'attributes.'</note>

<summary>This page argues that humans cannot be the cause of their own existence, as that would be a logical impossibility, and therefore a "First Cause" or "Primary Being" must exist. It begins to define this Being as an infinite and perfect entity that contains all possible attributes within its unity.</summary>

<keywords>First Cause, Primary Being, logic, existence, reason, infinity, perfection, essence, causality, Enlightenment philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical deduction of the attributes of the "first cause" (God). The previous page ended while discussing how a cause must be related to its essence; this page completes the word "properties" (Eigenschaften) and argues that a first cause must possess all perfections it bestows upon its effects.</meta>

**properties** <term>properties</term> <note>original: 'Eigenschaften'. In this context, the inherent characteristics or qualities that define a being.</note> and all **perfection** <term>perfection</term> <note>original: 'Vollkommenheit'. This refers to the state of being complete and lacking nothing; in 18th-century philosophy, if a cause creates something with a certain quality, the cause must already possess that quality in a higher degree.</note> from all the effects it has produced or can still produce; for a cause must necessarily possess all the properties of the beings it has produced, or is capable of producing, within itself. Otherwise, it would or could grant what it does not possess, nor could ever possess.

The **first cause** <term>first cause</term> <note>original: 'erste Ursache'. A philosophical concept, often associated with Aristotle's "unmoved mover," referring to the ultimate origin of all things that does not itself have a cause.</note> would simply not be perfect if it were not from **eternity** <term>eternity</term> <note>original: 'Ewigkeit'. Existing outside of time, without beginning or end.</note>, because it would otherwise have had a beginning and could have an end. For this reason, it would not be the first cause, as it would owe its **existence** <term>existence</term> <note>original: 'Daseyn'. Literally "being there."</note> to another that existed before it. Consequently, this cause—which we take to be the first—would only be a second one; it would be limited in its **essence** <term>essence</term> <note>original: 'Wesenheit'. The fundamental nature or "what-ness" of a thing.</note> and in its perfection, as well as in its duration, and would be subject to another. 

However, if we accept it as the first, then all others depend upon it and are thus subordinate; from this, it is quite clear that the quality of a first cause is inseparable from **self-power** <term>self-power</term> <note>original: 'Eigenmacht'. Also translated as autonomy or inherent power; the ability to exist and act by one's own nature without external influence.</note>, from eternity, from **infinity** <term>infinity</term> <note>original: 'Unendlichkeit'</note>, and from supreme power. Thus, one cannot admit a first cause without simultaneously recognizing the existence of a **God**.

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical deduction regarding the nature of the first cause (God). The previous page concluded by stating that if a first cause were not eternal, it would be limited in its essence and perfection. This page explores the implications of that cause’s freedom and its role as the blueprint and ultimate goal of the universe.</meta>

If the **first cause** <term>first cause</term> <note>original: 'erste Ursache'. The ultimate origin of all things.</note> was free during the **creation** <note>original: 'Erschaffung'</note> of the world, it follows that all things that were made were made with reason and **intellect** <note>original: 'Erkenntniß'</note>, and consequently according to a **pre-figuration** <note>original: 'Vorbildung'. A mental model or conceptual archetype existing before the physical object was created.</note> or a pattern. However, because the first cause acts in an unconstrained manner, it could take no pattern for its products from anything outside of itself, much less work according to a model different from its own **essence** <term>essence</term>. Consequently, God is not only the first **self-acting cause** <note>original: 'selbstwirkende Ursache'. A cause that initiates action by its own power rather than being moved by an external force.</note>, but also the **formal cause** <term>formal cause</term> <note>original: 'bildliche Ursache'. Also known as the exemplary cause; this suggests that God uses His own perfect nature as the "blueprint" or image for everything He creates.</note> of all created things.

---

According to this accepted principle, one must maintain that this first cause, which is God, is also the **final cause** <term>final cause</term> <note>original: 'Endursache'. The 'telos' or ultimate purpose for which something exists.</note> of all things. For after he brought forth the world as an independent, free, and all-wise cause, he also set for himself a **final purpose** <note>original: 'Endzweck'</note> appropriate to his dignity—that is, himself and his own honor. Thus, the first cause is necessarily also the ultimate final cause of its products.

man, enlightened by the Father of all Light <note>original: 'Vater alles Lichts'. A biblical reference (James 1:17) frequently used in Enlightenment and Masonic texts to refer to God as the source of reason and illumination.</note>, is capable of and can do all things, save for the act of creation itself.

Our senses are but tools of the soul; they are, so to speak, placed within a glorious sanctuary <term>sanctuary</term> <note>Likely referring to the body or the mind as a "temple" where the soul resides and processes sensory information.</note>. The soul receives their counsel and makes use of them to bring all things under its dominion. To where does the power of the soul not extend? At its voice, the lifeless canvas is animated <note>original: 'beseelet'. Literally "ensouled," suggesting that the artist’s soul breathes life into their work.</note>, marble and bronze breathe with life, the earth resounds with sweet song, and everything takes on a new form. Animals perceive this voice and obey it; some, despite their immense size, come willingly and docily offer their service to man—the elephant throws itself at his feet. Others, filled with fear, seek a secure refuge in vain within thick forests and dark, deep caves. The bird that traverses the air falls at his feet; the fleeting flight of animals upon the earth does not protect them against the arrow of pursuit, and the depths of the roaring sea no longer provide a sanctuary for the massive whale. — Yet even all this is far too little — the soul even commands the elements, and the elements obey it; the power of fire melts the har[dest]— <note>The word 'här-' is truncated at the end of the page; it likely continues as 'härtesten' (hardest) on the following page, referring to the melting of hard metals.</note>

From this principle—namely, that man commands everything on this earth and exercises such a wide-reaching dominion over all other beings because his soul, this noblest part of his essence, is the image of the <term>Most High</term> <note>original: 'Allerhöchsten', a superlative title for God used to emphasize His supreme authority.</note>—it follows quite clearly and irrefutably that this limited, yet so highly exalted being must relate itself to the <term>Author</term> <note>original: 'Urheber', here meaning the source or creator of human life and dignity.</note> of its elevation and existence. 

It follows, too, that this relationship is one of pure submissiveness; that submissiveness brings duties in its wake; that duties require <term>compulsion</term> <note>original: 'Zwang', implying a moral necessity or inner discipline rather than physical force.</note>; and that compulsion and reluctance must be conquered through virtue. However, although virtue is our first duty, our strongest inclination does not tend toward it. Even though virtue leads us to our happiness, one nonetheless finds—amidst its most glorious and lovely attractions—a certain hardness and severity, I know not what, which terrifies and outrages all passions. 

In the wake of the passions, there then follow baseness and self-contempt. One might wish to be great and glorious again, but to be freer in exchange. One goes so far as to prefer slavery to royal dignity; and in order to justify to oneself the shameful state in which one remains <term>hardened</term> <note>original: 'verstocket', a term often used in a theological sense to describe a heart that has become stubborn or insensitive to divine grace.</note>, one renounces

## On the Union of the Soul
### with the Body.

Although the various properties in every part of this universe reveal themselves in their mutual affinities and relationships, one must nonetheless confess that they appear in their fullest luster, especially in the union of the **soul** with the **body**

<summary>The author argues that denying the soul's divine origin reduces humans to the level of animals and critiques skeptical philosophers like Anaxagoras. The text then shifts to explore the mysterious relationship between the human soul and the physical body.</summary>
<keywords>human dignity, materialism, Creator, immortal soul, Anaxagoras, atheism, skepticism, body and soul</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of the human soul's role within the physical world. The previous page established that the union of the soul and body is where the wonders of the universe are most clearly seen, contrasting this with materialist views that lower humans to the level of animals.</meta>

to seem [so], because through this **operation** <note>original: 'Würkung'. In this context, it refers to the functional effect or divine influence of the soul acting within the physical world.</note> the entire physical order reaches its ultimate end-purpose: the glory of its **Author**. Through this, the whole world corresponds to the intentions of the one who drew it forth out of nothingness. Indeed, the more immeasurable and glorious this entire visible creation is, the more purposeless and unnecessary it would be if man had not been placed within it. Of what use were the size and splendor of this magnificent temple, if it had remained without a **High Priest**? <note>A common theological metaphor: the physical world is a 'temple' of God, but it requires a human 'High Priest' to consciously recognize and voice its praise.</note>

Only an infinite wisdom is capable of instilling a fervor of love into sluggish beings that are otherwise incapable of all sensation and love, and of producing in them a service of veneration and **gratitude**. This plan, which is beyond all created reason, was woven into the very formation of man and executed on that eternally remarkable day <note>A reference to the day of Creation in Genesis.</note>. In this new creature, the gifts of heaven and earth are now united; even **matter** itself—which is in and of itself lifeless, inactive, and sluggish—came to share in the service and the worship that man offered to the Most High. The songs of praise that his tongue voiced were like the voice of all nature; the Creator inclined

<summary>The author argues that humanity serves as the "High Priest" of the universe, providing a voice of gratitude for an otherwise silent material world. Through the union of soul and matter, the entire physical creation fulfills its ultimate purpose of glorifying God.</summary>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the unique union between the human soul and physical matter, framing it as the pinnacle of divine creation. It picks up mid-sentence from the previous page's description of God listening to man's praise.</meta>

[He inclined] his ear to this voice, and His divine eye surveyed with new pleasure <note>original: 'Wohlgefallen'. This term carries a theological weight, echoing the biblical sentiment of God seeing that His creation was "good."</note> the completed work of His creation.

The union of the **soul** with **matter**, which could only be designed by an unlimited **wisdom**, required an infinite **omnipotence** in order to be carried out. He must have drawn these substances forth from nothingness in order to be able to exercise this absolute power over them; the obstacles that each of these substances opposed to their mutual union were insurmountable to any other hand, except to His, which nothing resists.

This effect of omnipotence, so wonderful in itself, has other infinitely remarkable qualities that deserve our full attention, because through them we exist, and through them we live. If the constant continuation of our **existence** <note>original: 'Daseyn'. We ourselves are the most visible and greatest **monuments** <note>original: 'Denkmale'. Here used in the sense of 'living evidence' or 'testament' to divine skill.</note> of wisdom, of omnipotence, and of the

## Microcosmic Portrait.

I have shown earlier that our senses are the tools of the soul, and that God has granted humanity dominion over everything on earth; now I wish to show why man is called the little world (**Microcosm** <note>original: 'Microcosmus'. ## Notes

### on the Meyerian *acidum pingue* <term>acidum pingue: A 18th-century chemical theory proposed by Johann Friedrich Meyer, who believed a "fatty acid" or "fire-matter" was absorbed by limestone to make it caustic. This was a rival theory to the discovery of "fixed air" (carbon dioxide).</term>

If we more closely consider the separation of the waters under the firmament from the waters above the firmament (1): then it is to be suspected that these waters were only a watery vapor or a dark moisture; for if, among all created substances, moisture alone is capable of assuming all forms, it can therefore also be the substance from which the entire work of Creation is brought forth. <note>This reflects a common alchemical and pre-modern view that water or "moisture" was the *prima materia*, the original substance from which God shaped the world.</note>

Since this dark chaos was unformed and a confused mass—which is suited for all forms and yet indifferent to all—it necessarily had to have the essence of a moist vapor.

One always notices that in all products of this lower world, the seeds are surrounded by a watery matter, and that the seeds of the plant kingdom, when they into the

# Remarks

## regarding Meyer’s <term>fatty acid</term> <note>original: "acidum pingue." Johann Friedrich Meyer (1705–1779) was a German apothecary who proposed that "acidum pingue"—a fatty, fire-like acid—was the substance that transferred from fire into limestone to make it caustic (quicklime). This theory was a major rival to Joseph Black's discovery of carbon dioxide.</note>

With the assistance of nature, art demonstrates this as well in the animal kingdom: in the mineral kingdom, one finds that Mercury, mixed with Sulfur, is this first matter which is so greatly sought by the wise, and recognized only by true <term>philosophers</term> <note>In alchemical texts, "philosophers" refers to "true alchemists" or seekers of natural wisdom, rather than modern academic philosophers.</note>; they alone possess the key to it. Herein consists the true and proper <term>fatty acid</term> <note>original: "acidum pingue." The author is reclaiming this term from the chemist Johann Friedrich Meyer, arguing it is a spiritual substance rather than a caustic chemical.</note>, which will never be found in such bodies that have withstood the violence of fire, because it is impossible to find a life-giving essence in a body that has passed through corrosion or through melting; nature forces nothing by violence, as art does through chemistry.

We wish to dissolve, but we only lay the parts asunder; nature does exactly the opposite. She has only two operations: namely, she dissolves and she coagulates <note>original: "stockt zusammen." This refers to the fundamental alchemical maxim "solve et coagula"—the cycle of breaking down and rebuilding matter.</note>; she separates the pure from the impure; she only joins herself with parts of a like nature and rejects those that are of a foreign kind, whereas <term>art</term> <note>In this context, "Art" refers to human-made technology or experimental chemistry, which the author views as a crude imitation of natural processes.</note> mixes everything together without distinction and without knowledge of the cause. One can call nothing a dissolution except that which is of one and the same nature; for the gold-water (<term>water</term> <note>original: "aqua." [Aqua] regia) <note>The text completes the term "Aqua regia" (Royal Water) from the previous page. I define dissolution as occurring when two similar bodies of the same kind—such as ice in warm water, or salt dissolved in water—are joined, because these bodies are of a like kind, unite with one another, and accomplish a true dissolution <note>The author is using a pre-modern definition of "dissolution," arguing that a substance is only truly dissolved if it merges with a solvent of a similar spiritual essence, rather than being forced apart by a corrosive acid.</note>.

### Treatise
## on the Universal Medicine.

I have no doubt that the <term>hermetic philosophers</term> <note>Alchemists who follow the tradition of Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of the "hermetic" arts.</note>, who are initiated into the <term>spagyric mystery</term> <note>original: "spagyrischen Geheimniß." Spagyrics refers to the alchemical process of separating substances into their primary elements and then recombining them for medicinal use.</note>, will rise up against me because I speak with such clarity; they would indeed be right to do so, if the honor of God and the welfare of one’s neighbor did not deserve more consideration than their own persons. I speak only to the children of the true doctrine; only to them do I today unlock the secrets of hidden <term>worldly wisdom</term> <note>original: "Weltweisheit," a term often used in this period to denote philosophy or the deep study of natural laws.</note>, to let them see with their own eyes and grasp with their own hands the true interpretation of the writings of the Sages. These writings are the same ones which the inhabitants of the <term>chemical mountain</term> <note>original: "chimischen Bergs." This is a metaphorical location in alchemical literature, representing the difficult ascent toward the Great Work or the secret society of true adepts.</note> have used to hide their land from the ungodly, the sworn enemies of the living God, and the learned pupils of Nature. And since I have thereby discovered the truth of this art, you must confess that it is permissible, righteous, and virtuous, for David, Solomon, and <term>Ezra</term> <note>original: "Estrah." Biblical figures were frequently cited by alchemists to prove that their craft was a divine gift granted to the wise and holy men of the past.</note> bear witness to us of it.

<summary>The author begins a treatise on the Universal Medicine, justifying his decision to reveal alchemical secrets by arguing that helping one's neighbor and honoring God is more important than the traditional secrecy of the "Hermetic Philosophers."</summary>

...when you have opened the dungeon of Hippocrates <note>Hippocrates (c. 460 – 370 BC) was a Greek physician often called the "Father of Medicine." <meta>Page 45 continues the philosophical defense of the "Universal Medicine." The previous page concluded by defining this medicine as the "Azoth" or universal solvent. This page begins by asserting that this "true medicine" is the supreme discipline, surpassing and encompassing all other liberal arts and sciences. ...neither the **art of music** <note>original: *Tonkunst*; the study of harmony and sound</note> with the grace of its harmony, nor the **art of measurement** <note>original: *Meßkunst*; geometry</note> with its dimensions, nor **astronomy** <note>original: *Sternkunde*; the study of the stars</note> with its spheres—in short—I exclude no art or science from it. The **art of medicine** <note>original: *Arzneikunst*</note> is the most perfect model of all the wonders we possess; therefore, in a word, perfect medicine is a universal science—that is, the epitome of all sciences—so that it is impossible to separate one from the other without destroying the whole.

(This is much like the statues of the famous sculptor **Phidias** <note>The text uses the spelling "Chidias." Phidias (c. 480–430 BC) was the most celebrated sculptor of ancient Greece. Alchemical texts often cited him because of the legend that he crafted the statue of Athena with such integrated precision that his own likeness could not be removed from her shield without the entire statue falling apart—a metaphor for the unity of the "Great Work."</note>, whose memory antiquity has preserved even to our times, and which were constructed with such art that a single displaced stone caused the entire collapse). For this reason, the human spirit finds in medicine (that is, in the true kind) a type of peace that all other sciences grant only when taken together, and which each on its own could not produce. And since the art of medicine is the greatest joy of mankind, the only true good of life, and a gift from God, and all its effects are just as many miracles, even the wildest nations have loved its followers so much that they erected columns to them, just as they did for the immortal gods.

I say, therefore, that **Hermetic medicine** <note>original: *hermetisch Medizin*; a system of healing based on the teachings attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, emphasizing the chemical and spiritual transformation of matter.</note> is truly certain, because it was created by God and possesses its own very definite rules; for God and Nature (as **the Philosopher** <note>Usually a reference to Aristotle, who famously stated that "Nature does nothing in vain."</note> says) do nothing in vain. 

**The Preacher** <note>original: *Der Prediger*; here referring to the author of the Book of Sirach (also called Ecclesiasticus), specifically chapter 38, which is a classic defense of the physician’s craft.</note> teaches us this with these words: *The Lord has created medicine out of the earth* etc. and further down: *Was the bitter water not made sweet by the wood?* <note>A reference to Exodus 15:25, where Moses throws wood into bitter water to make it drinkable, interpreted here as an alchemical process.</note> its virtue is revealed so that men might recognize it: and throughout almost the entire chapter, the Wise Man speaks only of the excellence and truth of the **medical art** <note>original: *Arzneikunst*</note>, showing that God is the author of it, and consequently it is very true. 

Here one must note that he says the bitter water was sweetened by the wood, meaning to say that through the preparation of remedies—which are taken from the earth and used according to the prescription—diseases are quickly healed. This can be called the <term>ambrosia</term> <note>original Latin: *ambrosia*; in mythology, the food of the gods that granted immortality. In alchemy, it refers to a perfected medicinal substance.</note>, the **bread of the gods** <note>original: *Götterbrod*</note>, or the **Elixir of the Wise**, as you will find in my small treatise on *ambrosia* (translated from the English) at the end of this work.

O holy and wonderful Nature! says a philosopher, you never allow the **artist** <note>The German text ends with the fragment "Künst-", completed by the catchword "ler" as *Künstler*. In this context, the "artist" is the alchemist who works with nature.</note>

<summary>The author argues that Hermetic medicine is a divine science ordained by God, citing biblical passages to justify the alchemical preparation of earthly substances into life-prolonging elixirs like ambrosia.</summary>

## Fourfold Progression.

If man possessed a <term>chemical art</term> <note>original: "Scheidekunst." Those elements to which the name of "elements" is commonly given are not truly simple and uniform; they are mixed and bound together in such a way that they are inseparable, and although the author allows for only three, I venture to allow four. Here is the inseparable fourfold number, of which two are visible and two are invisible. However, the perceptible bodies of earth, water, and air, which are true and distinct in their kind, are not those primary elements that nature employs for the production of various things; they seem only to be the matter for the others; the simple elements are neither comprehensible nor tangible until their union produces a dense matter which we call a **body**, to which coarse matters are added as supplementary parts; the former are the **soul of the mixtures**, the active, the form-giving, or the acting, and the latter are only bodies—the enduring, the matter, or the passive <note>original: "das duldende... oder das leidende." In historical natural philosophy, these terms describe matter that receives action or form from an external "active" force.</note>.

When God had formed Nature to rule over the entire governance of the world, she began to assign to every thing a place and dignity according to its merit; and after she had brought forth the four <insert>elements</insert>, she ordered them so that one should act upon the other; from which then three <term>primary causes</term> <note>original: "Grundursachen." *   **Fire** began to act against the **Air**, and from this interaction **Sulfur** arose; 
*   Likewise, the **Air** began to act against the **Water**, from which **Mercury** <note>original: "Quecksilber." In this context, it refers to the philosophical principle of "Mercury" (representing liquidity and volatility) rather than the common metal.</note> arose; 
*   The **Water** likewise began to act against the **Earth**, from which **Salt** was produced. 

The author passes over the Air, which I shall treat in the following section.

### On Hidden Qualities.

What is hidden from physical eyes is that which they do not see; and what is hidden from the <term>intellect</term> <note>original: "Verstand." In this context, it refers to the higher faculty of reason or spiritual understanding, contrasted here with simple sensory perception.</note> is that which it does not comprehend; now, regarding this intellect, I ask: can anything be more hidden from the eyes than the generally accepted concepts regarding all the objects I have cited? They explain matter through matter; they explain man through the senses; they explain the <term>Author of all things</term> <note>original: "Urheber aller Dinge." A reference to God as the primary cause and creator of the universe.</note> through elemental nature.

Man sees the play of his organs within his senses, but he does not recognize his intellect within them.

In short, visible nature presents to the eyes the work of a great <term>Master Craftsman</term> <note>original: "Werkmeister." A common Enlightenment and alchemical metaphor for God as the supreme architect of the universe.</note>; however, if it does not offer the cause of things to the intellect, then it leaves us in uncertainty regarding the justice of the Master, the tenderness of a Father, and all the counsels of a Prince, so that one cannot deny that this explanation is entirely invalid and devoid of truth, because it always requires new explanations itself.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical investigation into human understanding. While the previous page established that the "Master Craftsman" is hidden from those who only look at the material surface, this page explores how the human intellect can bridge that gap through self-reflection and the analytical "art of resolution."</meta>

God has endowed all humanity with the same senses; from these, identical sensations arise, and consequently, identical concepts.

Yet nothing is as common as seeing poorly and understanding incorrectly. The only method belonging to man for forming a genuine judgment is the <term>art of resolution</term> <note>original: "Auflösungskunst." In this context, it refers to the analytical process of breaking down complex ideas or substances into their simplest parts to understand their true nature.</note>. And if this same person wishes to proceed at once from first principles with all their <term>intellect</term> <note>original: "Verstand."</note> to approach the Godhead, this can only occur step by step, by investigating the intellect itself through the nature of its own being. Has there ever been a person who could boast that their intellect alone proves the existence of their spiritual soul or their immortality? If one follows the intentions of the Creator very closely—intentions which shine forth everywhere—and especially if one turns inward to seek self-knowledge (whether regarding how life is received, maintained, or surrendered), then one will recognize a Supreme Head as the <term>First Cause</term> <note>original: "erste Ursache." A philosophical term for the primary origin of all things, usually identified as God.</note>, whom one must admire through His effects.

Two opposing passions contend for the human heart and lead it astray; pride, like a haughty eagle, seeks only to reach the heights.

<page-num>194</page-num>

It is no longer in your power to take away from him the strength you have given him; and far from wishing to do so, you now seek nothing else but the happy moment when your **brother**—who is also your **son** <note>In alchemy, the Stone is the "son" of gold because it is derived from it, but a "brother" because they share the same solar essence</note>—freed from the remains of his **impurities** <note>original: "Unreinigkeiten"; the dross or "feces" of the base metal that must be purged</note>, will reign in **glory**, and will also crown your other children <note>The "other children" are the lesser metals, which the Stone has the power to transmutate or "crown" into gold</note>.

**Diana** <note>The personification of the Moon and the alchemical principle of Silver</note> watched the rage of the Sun with unease. She was already in a rather peevish mood. For several months her hunt had been difficult; the frost and snow <note>Symbolic of the "cold" stages of the alchemical process, or perhaps the "White" stage (Albedo)</note> had often robbed her hounds of the scent of the forest's inhabitants. To bring her **cold melancholy** to its peak, she happened at that very moment to be an eyewitness to the unchaste embraces of **Mars** and **Venus** <note>Iron and Copper; their "embrace" signifies a chemical conjunction or the merging of these two metallic principles</note>; which they did even before the eyes and in the company of this chaste goddess. Since she wore nothing on her head but a light **half-moon** <note>The crescent symbol, the standard signature for Silver</note>, these deities—blinded by the violence of their desires—had no doubt failed to recognize the chaste Diana.

As the goddess still saw the **cold woman** <note>Likely referring to the unrefined matter or the "Lunar" state of the mercury</note> whom the gods threatened to make equal to her,

...seen, how the **red servant** <note>The "red servant" represents the Alchemical Stone in its final stage of perfection, known as the *Rubedo* or Redness.</note> had become equal to the **Sun**, she could not endure such a great pain; she forgot that she was a goddess and abandoned herself to the weakness of her sex. In this way, the cold woman became just as fortunate as the red servant had become, as did her male and female companions, who became ready for marriage and were capable of bringing forth the **King** and the **Queen**—that is, the **Heaven** and the **Earth**—purified and wedded to one another.

**Mercury** <note>The principle of Quicksilver, the messenger and mediator of the chemical wedding.</note> had succeeded too well not to complete his work; but because what remained to be done was merely **child's play and a women's pastime** <term>ludus puerorum; opus mulierum</term> <note>Famous alchemical phrases meaning the final stage of the process requires only gentle, steady heat and patience rather than laborious chemical intervention.</note>, and because he was otherwise called by **Jupiter** for an embassy, he entrusted the remainder of his work to his mother, **Maya**.

<meta>This page continues the allegorical narrative of the alchemical "Chemical Wedding." Mercury has handed over the final, gentle stage of the process—often called "women's work" in alchemical texts—to a feminine principle (Maya) to achieve the final perfection of the Stone.</meta>

<page-num>196</page-num>

...to **Maya** <note>In this context, Maya likely refers to the Earth or a personification of the formative power of nature.</note>, who brought it to perfection at her **spinning wheel** <note>The image of spinning signifies the cyclical nature of the alchemical process and the "weaving" together of elements.</note>, by simply taking care to keep the **cold woman** and the **red servant**—whom I shall henceforth call the **King** and the **Queen**, and whom **Mercury** had enclosed in a **crystalline palace** <note>A common alchemical metaphor for the glass flask or "philosopher's egg" in which the materials are heated.</note>—in a gentle heat.

Finally, **Mercury** made from the body of the **King** the **Tincture of the Sages**; this was the **ambrosia** <note>The food of the gods, here representing the life-giving, transformative power of the Philosophers' Stone.</note> of the divine feast at the wedding celebration of **Heaven** and **Earth**, which concluded at that moment.

<summary>This page concludes an alchemical allegory where the "King" and "Queen" (Sulfur and Mercury) undergo a process of dissolution and rebirth within a glass vessel. The result is the "Tincture of the Sages," the final Philosophers' Stone, which symbolizes the union of Heaven and Earth.</summary>

[Ju]piter was satisfied with the work of the **son of Maya** <note>Mercury (Hermes), the son of the nymph Maia in Greco-Roman mythology, here serving as the messenger and mediator of the alchemical process.</note>; and to show him his satisfaction with this, he permitted **Mercury** to increase the **Tincture of the Sages** <term>Tincture: The final alchemical elixir or Philosopher’s Stone, capable of transmuting base metals and restoring health.</term> by ten, and ten times ten, and ten times a hundred, and ten times a thousand, both in power and in quantity, by simply allowing the **King** and the **Queen**, his wife, to bathe in the **blood of the red servants** and the **cold women**, whom **Mercury** had kept within the **serpents of his staff** <note>The Caduceus, Mercury's staff entwined with two snakes, representing the balance of opposing forces (sulfur and mercury).</note>, and to whom the **King** and the **Queen** imparted kingdoms as great as their own. Whatever concepts one may have regarding <term>vegetation</term><note>In the 18th century, "vegetation" often referred to the internal biological process of growth and the "life force" of plants rather than just a collection of flora.</note>, one must always have recourse to water for the development of the <term>germ</term><note>The original German "Keim" refers to the embryo or sprout within a seed.</note> of all plants. One perceives how necessary they find it for their growth and prosperity, and everyone knows that a plant is lost beyond all hope as soon as it is deprived of moisture.

Therefore, not just any and all moisture without distinction makes the soil fertile; it must specifically be that of water. Plants require a nourishment that they can easily transform into their own nature. Firstly, this nourishment must be susceptible to all sorts of shapes, for it must take on the form of the various parts with which it unites. Secondly, it must be a substance that has no taste of its own, so that plants can impart their own to it. Since every plant has its particular taste, which is unique in its kind, it is much easier for it to give that taste to a substance that has none at all, rather than to change a taste in a substance that already possesses one. <meta>This page continues the author's argument from page 91/92. ...which one evaluates according to the amount of saltpeter they contain; see how this half-rotted manure is thrown from the stables, which contains a great quantity of the salts provided to us by the animals. It lets a brownish water trickle down, thickened by all the salts it has dissolved. If one were to throw the most suitable seeds for germination into this water, they would remain motionless, or rather destroy and consume themselves than grow. Here, there is a lack of neither heat nor salts, for a great quantity of them is present; instead, the lack is of water. All this moisture is saturated <note>Original: "geschwängert," literally "impregnated" or "pregnant," used here in the chemical sense of being heavily laden with a substance.</note> and filled with salt. Every water particle is already at the point called saturation, and salts are still present in abundance which, if new water were added, could seize hold of it; therefore, none remains that would be capable of penetrating the pores <term>pores (Poros)</term><note>The author uses the Latinate "Poros" to refer to the microscopic openings in plant tissue through which nutrients and water were thought to pass.</note> of the plants. It lets a brownish water trickle down, thickened by all the salts it has dissolved. If one were to throw the most suitable seeds for germination into this water, they would remain motionless, or rather destroy and consume themselves than grow. 

Here, there is a lack of neither heat nor salts, for a great quantity of them is present; instead, the lack is of water. All this moisture is saturated <note>Original: "geschwängert," literally "impregnated," used here in the chemical sense of being heavily laden with a substance.</note> and filled with salt. Every water particle is already at the point called saturation, and salts are still present in abundance which, if new water were added, could seize hold of it; therefore, none remains that would be capable of penetrating the pores <term>pores (Poros)</term> of the plants. 

The shape that these salts have given to the water globules <term>globules (Wasserkügelchen)</term><note>An 18th-century scientific concept where water was thought to be composed of tiny spherical particles that could be reshaped or "weighted down" by dissolved minerals.</note> creates an obstacle; their thickening offers resistance; the delicate fibers <note>The "fibers" (Fibern) refer to the microscopic structural and vascular components of the plant roots.</note> cannot absorb the water particles that are so heavily laden; their taste, their smell, their color—everything resists; everything makes them unfit for vegetation <note>Used here in the historical sense of "the process of growing as a plant."</note>: there must be an excess of moisture,

...then they would possess the properties of these salts, and all would share the same smell and the same taste. One will object to me: plants yield a salt which they contain, and which they consequently must have received; and therefore it seems that salts increase fertility, or at least contribute to its production. Although water is the true fundamental cause of the fertility of the soil; yet, on the other hand, the soil must also be capable of absorbing and retaining it. It must contribute to this by being, as it were, its *placenta* <note>original: "Placenta." If we walk through the fields, experience will confirm this conclusion tangibly and beyond contradiction; sand is a composition of stones, or of small, very noticeable pebbles. Each of these small bodies is very hard and cannot be penetrated by water, so that even the most frequent rains and the most abundant dews on a field of this kind only moisten the surfaces of each grain of sand, and the liquid itself thus flows immediately and without hindrance between these small bodies; their hardness does not allow for that type of joining, doughy consistency, and certain <term>gluing together (Zusammenleimung)</term> <note>The author is likely describing what we now call soil structure or "tilth," particularly the way clay or organic matter helps soil particles stick together to hold moisture.</note> which occurs in other soils. Those bodies which only hold water on their surfaces lose their moisture at the slightest wind or the most imperceptible heat. <meta>This page continues the discussion from page 96 regarding how different soil types interact with water. While the previous page focused on sand, this page addresses larger stony materials and dense, cohesive soils (likely clay).</meta>

A stony field consists of hard, uneven, and larger bodies than those mentioned before. Here too, there are many stones that do not absorb water and can only retain it on their surface; they are dried even more quickly than sand. Thus, stony ground is unfit for <term>vegetation (Vegetation)</term> <note>Referring to the active process of plant growth and the life-cycle of the plant.</note> due to a lack of water.

Let us consider a soil where the opposite is the case; there is, for example, one which has this consistency, this cohesion, which—because its parts are very closely joined—retains water for a very long time. It requires more labor; one must divide its parts more often <note>The author is likely describing the need for intensive tilling or plowing to break up heavy, compacted soil like clay.</note>; either so that the water may penetrate to the small roots, or so that the very delicate and fine fibers can expand and lengthen without much resistance. In such a place, you will also have fertility, provided you have water.

Without going through every type of field, it is enough to have examined that every soil is more or less fertile according to how closely it approaches the aforementioned nature of soils—that is, in proportion to whether it can retain more or less water. If one gives to the sandy or stony soil—

Various naturalists, who were also concerned with agriculture, recognized several types of manure, among which they particularly praised <term>marl (Mergel)</term> <note>A crumbly earthy deposit consisting of clay and calcium carbonate, used as a fertilizer for lime-deficient soils.</note>; however, when it is chemically analyzed, it often contains less <term>saltpeter (Salpeter)</term> <note>Potassium nitrate; in early modern science, it was often believed to be the "essential salt" or life-force responsible for plant growth.</note> than the soil it is intended to make fertile. In this capacity, it is not actually manure; it only preserves it. The very same can be said of <term>lime (Kalk)</term> <note>Calcium oxide or hydroxide, used to reduce soil acidity and improve structure.</note>, which tends to bind and, as it were, glue together the particles it touches. It is this very adhesion that holds the water back in a thousand small containers.

<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of water as the primary driver of fertility, moving from the specific example of the Nile to a general theory of plant physiology and the physical properties of water.</meta>

It is correct and proven by constant experience that wherever one provides enough of this fundamental cause, rich vegetation is also produced; one even brings forth great meadows upon cliffs if water is directed there and retained; however, one observation must be made: the nourishment so necessary to the life and growth of animals becomes, when consumed in too great a quantity, so harmful that the excess even causes death for them.

Water is the nourishment of <term>plants</term> <note>Original: "Vegetabilien." <meta>This page continues the argument that water is the fundamental source of all life and fertility. While the previous page focused on how water sustains plants regardless of soil or manure, this page addresses exceptions to the rule and expands the theory to include the animal kingdom and northern climates.</meta>

It is true that there are waters which do not produce fertility; perhaps because they carry certain dissolved salts with them, or because the <term>sediment</term> <note>Original: "Satz." In 18th-century natural history, this refers to the solid matter or mineral deposits suspended in water that were thought to settle and form the "body" of plants.</note> they contain is composed of particles that are too large or too hard. Or, finally, it may be because this water—much like the salts themselves—has already taken on certain properties that are too difficult to alter.

Not only for the plant kingdom, but also for the animal kingdom, water is infinitely fertile. No animal produces as many <term>offspring</term> <note>Original: "Brut."</note> as fish; in some, one finds more eggs than in ten thousand land animals combined. Procreation in water is always very great, provided the water has not attracted any foreign or harmful properties.

In short, everywhere water is the primary cause of fertility. <term>Geography</term> <note>Original: "Erdbeschreibung." Literally "description of the earth," a term used before the modern academic discipline of geography was fully standardized.</note> shows us no cold country without reporting on its fertility; and in all these northern regions where moisture can remain—where the soil, covered for so long with snow, retains the fertilizing water—men gather abundant harvests. In the wildest regions, the earth brings forth numerous forests of its own accord, and these

Trees that grow without care and without concern <note>Original: "ohne Sorge." Fertility appears wherever there is water. On the contrary, in warm countries where the sun dries out the earth and robs it of moisture, one encounters nothing but parched deserts on which nothing thrives. From all this, it follows finally that warm and cold countries, and both firm and loose soil, are fertile only in proportion to how well their surfaces can retain water. Experience proves this everywhere and forces me to draw the conclusion that water is the true <term>solvent</term> <note>Original: "Auflösungsmittel." In the natural philosophy of this era, a solvent was the medium that broke down matter into its constituent parts so that it could be recombined into new life.</note> of bodies, and consequently the source of fertility.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion from page 111 regarding the nature of life and matter. Having previously argued that the "principles" of life are unique to each being and not simply transferred from food, the author now explains how the physical body is nonetheless sustained by the transformation of material particles.</meta>

Every individual being exists potentially in the material world before it has even emerged in a specific, individual form; and in its own time, it will return again to its order, and back to the very same point from which it originated.

Der Mensch <note>The catchword "D" from the previous page matches the start of this section.</note> requires nourishment, and only that which possesses some <term>vital spirit</term> <note>Original: "Lebensgeist." In 18th-century physiology, this referred to a subtle fluid or force believed to animate living organisms.</note> can provide him with food. For this purpose, he is served by roots, plants, legumes, seeds, and meat. Everything occurs with the assistance of <term>corpuscles</term> <note>Original: "Körperchen." This refers to the "corpuscularian" theory of matter, where small particles or atoms combine to form larger structures.</note> that pass from one composition to another.

This nourishment is necessary to increase the growth of a newborn child. Therefore, one must use a new mixture which—in order to provide food for the being that has just come into the world—must perish and be dissolved. Milk is very well suited for this, as evidenced by the <term>cycle</term> <note>Original: "Kreislauf," meaning circulation or cycle.</note> of these complex things that transform one into the other, while through all these manifold changes, the primary elements always remain the same.

Nature is therefore nothing other than a universal **cycle**, not through principles that have never acted (as our author says), but through the preservation of individual parts, which consists in the close connection of **form** and **matter**; the bond, the knot of this connection, is the **innate fire** <note>Original: "eingeborne Feuer." The insight of the ancient **world-sages** <note>Original: "Weltweisen," referring to the philosophers of antiquity, often implying those with alchemical or Hermetic knowledge.</note> penetrated into the most secret spiritual driving forces of this Nature, discovered, and found the heavenly and super-heavenly influences which the Most High had infused into all His works from the very beginning of the world. They thought that the spiritual principle of life was present in the nature of every being not only for its existence and preservation, but also for its **restoration**; and according to the principle of the sages: 

-> Nature contains nature, nature rejoices in nature, nature surpasses nature, nature is only improved in its own nature. <- <note>This is a famous alchemical axiom often attributed to pseudo-Democritus (c. 200 BCE). --- Page 153 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the spiritual "bonds" of life (innate fire and radical moisture) to the physical manifestation of those principles in the human body. It defines the human as a "microcosm" whose health is determined by the quality of the earthly matter they consume.</meta>

Every individual carries their death and their life within themselves, just as they carry sickness and health; only that which shares the same kind or quality as the individual can be considered healthy or sick.

We see an example of this in the human being. Regarding their external existence, man is formed from the finest clay of the earth—an extract of all earthly creations. For this reason, he is also called the **Microcosm**, or a "little world." <note>The concept of the Microcosm (the human) reflecting the Macrocosm (the universe) was central to Renaissance philosophy and alchemy, suggesting that the human body contains all the elements and principles of the wider world.</note>

Furthermore, everything that a person eats and drinks derives its form largely from the earth. The fruits that the earth generates, produces, and offers for nourishment are the primary causes of both sickness and health. The "nobler" <note>In this context, "noble" (German: *edel*) refers to the purity or spiritual refinement of a substance—its proximity to a perfected state—rather than social status.</note> the fruits or creations of the earth one consumes, the healthier one becomes. Conversely, the baser and poorer the nourishment, the sicker and weaker one remains. The former relate to the health and life of the body; the latter to its illness and death.

## System of Developments.

> “I have only one small remark regarding the observations of nature, and a word to say about a term which some apply when they discuss bodies. They designate, by the word **development** <term>development: German *Entwickelung*; in the 18th century, this often referred to the theory of 'Preformationism,' the idea that an organism exists fully formed but microscopic within the seed and merely 'unfolds' or expands.</term>, the **origin** and **growth**. We cannot allow this expression to pass; for if it were true that bodies merely developed, they would have to be complete within their germs, or within their principles; they would cause their original nature—which is simple—to vanish; then they would no longer be indivisible, and consequently not immortal; or, to maintain them thus within these principles, one would also have to assume the existence of corporeal <note>Physical or material beings.</note> beings contained within them; that would mean admitting what we have hitherto denied, and grossly contradicting what we formerly established as a truth.”

The author uses the word **shell** <note>German: *Hülle*. ### System on the Nature of Beings
Should one perhaps be allowed to exchange this expression for the word **expansion**? This would perhaps be more appropriate in his <note>Referring to the author being discussed or the specific philosophical system under review.</note> opinion.

I have stated above, both in the section where I dealt with **vegetation** <term>vegetation: In this historical context, the term refers to the general process of plant growth and the vital forces of life.</term>, as well as elsewhere, that everything returns to its **primary matter** <term>primary matter: German *Grundstoff*; the fundamental substance from which all things are composed and to which they return upon decay.</term>, and that every individual being exists according to its force, or capacity, within this material world even before it has emerged in a specific, individual **form**.

Now I wish to explain it either through **expansion** or through **development**, and in another way.

I place whatever grain (be it cereal or another seed) into the earth; it decays and dissolves through a dissolving agent which contains a **spirit**, whose components are for the most part of the same nature as those of the planted grain; or at least they are delicate and fine enough to penetrate into the empty spaces of the grain-husk and the seed, in which the **seed-spirit** <note>The vital, non-material force believed to reside within a seed and drive its growth.</note> is enclosed, which,

# On the Air.

> "I have noted that the air is not to be counted among the number of the elements; because one cannot, in truth, regard this coarse, fluid being—which we breathe, and which causes bodies to swell or contract according to whether it contains more or less water or fire—as a distinct element. <note>In traditional Aristotelian physics, air is one of the four elements. Here, the author argues it is a medium or essence rather than a building block.</note>
>
> "There is certainly a fundamental essence <term>Grundwesen|A term used in German alchemy and philosophy to describe the underlying spiritual or vital substance of a thing.</term> in this fluid that we should call 'air'; however, it is incomparably more active and powerful than the coarse and earthly elements of which bodies are composed. This air originated from fire—though not from the material fire we know, but rather from that [fire] which brought forth fire itself and all sensible things. <note>The author distinguishes between common fire and 'Celestial' or 'Philosophic' fire, a common distinction in Hermetic thought.</note> In a word, air is absolutely necessary for the preservation and life of all elemental bodies; they last no longer than it exists. However, because it is not a 'matter' in the same way they are, it cannot be regarded as an element; consequently, the proposition is true that it contributes nothing to the composition of these very bodies."

<meta>This page continues the philosophical and theological exploration of human nature. The first human, created in innocence and righteousness, knew no danger. The sense of immortality with which he was endowed raised him far above the fear of death and the various accidents that might bring it about.

He saw himself showered with the blessings of his Creator; the animals were his natural servants, intended for his service and for his pleasure <note>original: "Unterhaltung" — in this context, it implies both companionship and the enjoyment of nature.</note>. As the King of Nature, what had he to fear from the objects surrounding him, over which he ruled as an absolute master? Fear and shame only entered his experience through his own guilt <note>A reference to the biblical Fall of Man, where sin introduced mortality and fear.</note>.

In the present day, most people have sunk so low toward the state of animals that they grant the human race nothing more than a "material soul" <note>original: "materielle Seele" — a critique of Materialism, the philosophical view that nothing exists except matter and that the soul is not a spiritual, immortal entity.</note>. This state compels them to constantly seek only what flatters their self-love <note>original: "Eigenliebe" — often used in 18th-century philosophy to describe a narrow, selfish egoism as opposed to healthy self-preservation.</note>. Such people conflate all social classes and offices; they denounce subjection as a barbaric law, obedience as a weakness, and sovereign power as tyranny. They build their entire systems of thought upon nothing but obscure and purely imaginary foundations.

### On the Power of Man.

> "One also recognizes what infinite advantages would arise for the judge if he were the true instrument of this sensible, temporal, and universal cause; in it, he would find a certain light with which he could distinguish the innocent from the accomplice without error.

> "Therefore, in the eyes of the judge himself, one of his most important duties is to seek to recognize the truth in the giving of testimony; but how can he carry this out without the aid of that light which I point out to him, and which, as his only guiding star <note>original: *Leitstern*</note>, should constantly accompany him."

People are as they have been raised, and they would infallibly be better if they were better instructed: the means to make them just would be to impart to them concepts of justice. Jurisprudence <term>jurisprudence: the formal study or science of law</term> would then become unnecessary.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "single thing" or root matter mentioned by Hermes on the previous page. The text uses the metaphor of a tree and a tailor’s craft to explain the alchemical process of "solve et coagula" (dissolve and coagulate), specifically regarding the nature of Philosophical Mercury.</meta>

<page-num>146</page-num>

It <note>Refers back to the "single thing" or "medicine" mentioned by Hermes on page 145</note> resembles a tree whose branches, leaves, flowers, and fruits are from it, in it, with it, and through it.

All authors agree on this point: that in Mercury resides everything that the world-sages <note>original: *Weltweisen*; a term for philosophers or alchemists</note> seek. However, one is not to understand this as common mercury <term>common mercury: ordinary quicksilver or the liquid metal found in nature, as opposed to the "Philosophical Mercury" which alchemists believed was a spiritual essence hidden within matter</term>.

The World-Citizen <note>original: *Weltbürger*; a reference to "Cosmopolite," the pseudonym of the famous alchemist Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636)</note> supports this truth with the assertion that out of one comes two, and out of two comes one, and no more; this is the end goal toward which all philosophical operations <term>philosophical operations: the specific laboratory and meditative processes used by alchemists to transform matter</term> are directed.

A tailor will make a garment from a single piece of cloth; he will divide the cloth and afterwards join it back together so that he may produce the body and the sleeves, which all have particular names: yet the whole has arisen from this single piece, even though the specific parts bear different names; it is exactly the same in the art of medicine <term>art of medicine: original *Arzneykunde*; here referring to the preparation of alchemical remedies or the "Universal Medicine"</term>. This is what the author wishes to speak of: one must therefore separate what is united, and unite what is separated.

## On Immaterial Motion

> "Although in the visible world, motion and <term>extension</term> <note>original: *Ausdehnung*. ## On the Circular Line.

> "In fact, as new and strange as this may seem, I cannot help but confess that **extension** <note>original: *Ausdehnung*. As established previously, this refers to the physical property of occupying space.</note> and the **circular line** are one and the same thing; that is to say, that there is no extension except by means of the circular line, and conversely, that there is no circular line that is physical and visible—that is, that material and extended nature could only be formed from lines which are not straight, or what amounts to the same thing, that there is no such thing as a single **straight line** in nature.
