Which diversity in the works of the Creator; is **reason** here upon a throne? Does it exercise unrestricted dominion over the **senses**? When its torch is extinguished, do we have another whose light shall illuminate us? We are on this earth as if in a **hospital** <note>original: *Spital*; in this context, it refers to an asylum or madhouse where "fools" are kept.</note>, in which the fools of the entire world reside. Reason therein is nonsensical, and often plays the role of **folly**.

The **optical glasses** <term>optical glasses: *optische Gläser*, a reference to the then-recent invention of microscopes and telescopes which revealed worlds previously invisible to the naked eye.</term> have revealed to our eyes, with astonishment, the existence of infinitely small beings which we would never have suspected, and yet the **imagination** can never follow the reason which sees and proves them.

The error will always be in deficiency, and never in excess. What effect can possibly seem too great when one considers its cause?

<meta>This page continues the critique of a specific author's geometrical theory—likely Louis-Claude de Saint-Martin or a similar figure—specifically refuting the idea that straight lines are exclusive to the "quaternary" (the number four). ...say that there is a **center-point**, or a **circumference**; and just as a **diameter**, which forms two **radii** <note>original: *Halbmesser*</note>, is a straight line, one must allow for its existence, because it is one of the immediate productions <note>original: *Produktionen*; here meaning the geometric manifestations or outgrowths of spiritual principles</note> of the center-point toward the circumference.

The circumference consists of six equilateral triangles. The triangles consisting of straight lines—and these triangles which are divisible almost into infinity—will always consist of straight lines; the author is therefore mistaken when he says that straight lines exist only in the **quaternary number** <note>original: *vierfache Zahl*; the number four, which in this tradition represents the material realization of divine ideas</note>.

He makes squares. One cannot deny that these are likewise composed of straight lines; that in all squares there are diagonals which form triangles: consequently, the straight line exists—regardless of the author's <term>system of thought</term> <note>original: *Lehrgebäude*; literally a "teaching-building," referring to a structured philosophical or theoretical framework</note>—in all productions that exist in nature.

<summary>This page argues that straight lines are fundamental to all geometric and natural forms, refuting a theory that limits them to the number four. It demonstrates this by pointing to the diameters of circles and the diagonals of squares.</summary>

## Concerning the
# Changes of Nature.

---

> "What would enlighten man regarding this, is that through the contemplation of the **four realms** <note>original: *vier Reiche*; in the context of 18th-century mystical philosophy, these usually refer to the spiritual and material divisions of the universe</note>, of which we speak, he would see that there is one which suffers, one which receives, and two which act in opposition: he would thereby see that the misfortunes <note>original: *Unglücksfälle*; refers here to natural catastrophes, disruptions, or "disorders" in the physical world</note> whose traces the earth shows everywhere, belong necessarily to the effect of the two active realms set against one another—namely, the realm where **fire** rules, and that where **water** rules: he would then no longer ascribe the effects, of which his eyes are daily witnesses, to the element alone which seems to produce them, because he would recognize that these **changes** <note>original: *Veränderungen*; the central theme of this section, referring to the shifting state of the physical world</note> are the result of the constant conflict <note>original: *Streit*; a dialectical struggle or tension between opposing cosmic principles</note> of these two enemies, in which conflict now this one, now that one, gains the upper hand; but in which also

<term>Changes of Nature</term> <note>The physical transformations, disasters, and cycles observed in the material world.</note>, <term>four realms</term> <note>A classification of existence where different principles (suffering, receiving, acting) interact.</note>, **Fire**, **Water**, **Elements**, **Conflict of the enemies**.

> "one of the two cannot be the victor,
> "without the place on earth where the
> "struggle occurred suffering proportionately,
> "receiving hardship and changes."

The author implies through the **four realms** <note>original: *vier Reiche*; referring to the spiritual and material divisions of nature common in Martinist philosophy</note>, and through **action and reaction** <note>original: *Wirkung und Gegenwirkung*; a concept of dynamic balance</note>, that he admits **air** (as an element) and **fire** as an action against **water** and **earth**. This is because fire alone, without a medium—which is the air—could not act against water, as it must take from both the one part and the other; one is therefore forced to accept it as an element which belongs to the other three.

If fire acted entirely alone, without an **intermediary** <note>original: *Mittelding*; literally a "middle-thing" or mediating substance</note>, the effect, if it were not tempered, would be too violent. Since two extremes can only unite through intermediaries, **water** and **air**—because they share a similarity with fire and with earth—are the two intermediaries that Nature employs in the action and reaction of individual **sublunary** <term>sublunary: literally "below the moon"; a traditional term for the physical, earthly world where things are subject to change and decay, as opposed to the unchanging heavens</term> beings.
